<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Guinée" redirects here. For the concept in the African diasporic religion, see <a href="/wiki/Haitian_Vodou" title="Haitian Vodou">Haitian Vodou</a>.</div>

src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/11px-Decrease2.svg.png" decoding="async" title="Decrease" width="11" height="11" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/17px-Decrease2.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/22px-Decrease2.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="300" data-file-height="300" />&#160;0.465<sup id="cite_ref-HDI_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HDI-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><br /><span class="nowrap"><span style="color:red">low</span></span>&#160;·&#160;<a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index" title="List of countries by Human Development Index">182nd</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Currency</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Guinean_franc" title="Guinean franc">Guinean franc</a> (<a href="/wiki/ISO_4217" title="ISO 4217">GNF</a>)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Time zone</th><td class="infobox-data"><span class="nowrap"><a href="/wiki/Coordinated_Universal_Time" title="Coordinated Universal Time">UTC</a>&#32;</span> (<a href="/wiki/Greenwich_Mean_Time" title="Greenwich Mean Time">GMT</a>)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Left-_and_right-hand_traffic" title="Left- and right-hand traffic">Driving side</a></th><td class="infobox-data">right</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Guinea" title="Telephone numbers in Guinea">Calling code</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/%2B224" class="mw-redirect" title="+224">+224</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/ISO_3166" title="ISO 3166">ISO 3166 code</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/ISO_3166-2:GN" title="ISO 3166-2:GN">GN</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Country_code_top-level_domain" title="Country code top-level domain">Internet TLD</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/.gn" title=".gn">.gn</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Guinea</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span></span>/</a></span>&#32;<span class="nowrap" style="font-size:85%">(<span class="unicode haudio"><span class="fn"><span style="white-space:nowrap;margin-right:.25em;"><a href="/wiki/File:En-us-Guinea.ogg" title="About this sound"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/11px-Loudspeaker.svg.png" decoding="async" width="11" height="11" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/17px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/22px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="20" data-file-height="20" /></a></span><a href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/1f/En-us-Guinea.ogg" class="internal" title="En-us-Guinea.ogg">listen</a></span></span>)</span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">GHIN</span>-ee</i></a>),<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> officially the <b>Republic of Guinea</b> (<a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>: <i lang="fr">République de Guinée</i>), is a coastal country in <a href="/wiki/West_Africa" title="West Africa">West Africa</a>. It borders the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Ocean" title="Atlantic Ocean">Atlantic Ocean</a> to the west, <a href="/wiki/Guinea-Bissau" title="Guinea-Bissau">Guinea-Bissau</a> to the northwest, <a href="/wiki/Senegal" title="Senegal">Senegal</a> to the north, <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a> to the northeast, <a href="/wiki/Cote_d%27Ivoire" class="mw-redirect" title="Cote d&#39;Ivoire">Cote d'Ivoire</a> to the southeast, and <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone" title="Sierra Leone">Sierra Leone</a> and <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a> to the south. It is sometimes referred to as <b>Guinea-Conakry</b> after its capital <a href="/wiki/Conakry" title="Conakry">Conakry</a>, to distinguish it from other territories in the <a href="/wiki/Guinea_(region)" title="Guinea (region)">eponymous region</a> such as Guinea-Bissau and <a href="/wiki/Equatorial_Guinea" title="Equatorial Guinea">Equatorial Guinea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-uiowa1_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-uiowa1-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> It has a population of 13.5 million and an area of 245,857 square kilometres (94,926&#160;sq&#160;mi).<sup id="cite_ref-guinea_stats_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guinea_stats-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Formerly <a href="/wiki/French_Guinea" title="French Guinea">French Guinea</a>, it achieved independence in 1958.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> It has a history of military <a href="/wiki/Coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="Coup d&#39;état">coups d'état</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bariyo_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bariyo-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Larson_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Larson-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Paquett_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Paquett-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> After decades of authoritarian rule, in 2010 it held its first <a href="/wiki/Democracy" title="Democracy">democratic</a> <a href="/wiki/Elections_in_Guinea" title="Elections in Guinea">election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Paquett_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Paquett-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FH2010_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FH2010-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> As it continued to hold multi-party elections, the country continued to face ethnic conflicts, corruption, and abuses by military and police.<sup id="cite_ref-FH2010_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FH2010-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> In 2011, the United States government claimed that torture by security forces and abuse of women and children (including <a href="/wiki/Female_genital_mutilation" title="Female genital mutilation">female genital mutilation</a>) were ongoing human rights issues.<sup id="cite_ref-State_2011_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-State_2011-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In 2021, a <a href="/wiki/2021_Guinean_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="2021 Guinean coup d&#39;état">military faction overthrew president Alpha Condé</a> and suspended the constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-Bariyo_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bariyo-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Larson_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Larson-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Paquett_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Paquett-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Muslims represent 85% of the population.<sup id="cite_ref-uiowa1_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-uiowa1-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The country is divided into four geographic regions: <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Guinea" title="Maritime Guinea">Maritime Guinea</a> on the Atlantic coast, the <a href="/wiki/Fouta_Djallon" title="Fouta Djallon">Fouta Djallon</a> or Middle Guinea highlands, the <a href="/wiki/Upper_Guinea" title="Upper Guinea">Upper Guinea</a> savanna region in the northeast, and the <a href="/wiki/Guin%C3%A9e_foresti%C3%A8re" title="Guinée forestière">Guinée forestière</a> region of tropical forests. French, the official language of Guinea, is a language of communication in schools, in government administration, and the media. More than 24 indigenous languages are spoken and the largest are <a href="/wiki/Susu_language" title="Susu language">Susu</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pular_language" title="Pular language">Pular</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Maninka_language" title="Maninka language">Maninka</a>, which dominate respectively in Maritime Guinea, Fouta Djallon, and Upper Guinea, while Guinée forestière is ethnolinguistically diverse. Guinea's economy is mostly dependent on agriculture and mineral production.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> It is the world's second largest producer of <a href="/wiki/Bauxite" title="Bauxite">bauxite</a>, and has deposits of diamonds and gold.<sup id="cite_ref-guineaconakrysupport.com_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guineaconakrysupport.com-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> The country was at the core of the <a href="/wiki/2014_Ebola_outbreak" class="mw-redirect" title="2014 Ebola outbreak">2014 Ebola outbreak</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Name">Name</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Name">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Guinea_(region)#Etymology" title="Guinea (region)">Guinea (region) §&#160;Etymology</a></div>
<p>Guinea is named after the <a href="/wiki/Guinea_(region)" title="Guinea (region)">Guinea region</a> which lies along the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Guinea" title="Gulf of Guinea">Gulf of Guinea</a>. It stretches north through the <a href="/wiki/Tropical_and_subtropical_moist_broadleaf_forests" title="Tropical and subtropical moist broadleaf forests">forested tropical regions</a> and ends at the <a href="/wiki/Sahel" title="Sahel">Sahel</a>. The English term Guinea comes directly from the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_language" title="Portuguese language">Portuguese</a> word <i>Guiné</i> which emerged in the mid-15th century to refer to the lands inhabited by the <i>Guineus</i>, a generic term for the black African peoples south of the <a href="/wiki/Senegal_River" title="Senegal River">Senegal River</a>, in contrast to the "tawny" Zenaga Berbers above it, whom they called <i>Azenegues</i> or <a href="/wiki/Moors" title="Moors">Moors</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Guinea" title="History of Guinea">History of Guinea</a></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .mbox-text-span{margin-left:23px!important}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .ambox{margin:0 10%}}</style><table class="box-More_citations_needed plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.<br /><small><span class="plainlinks"><i>Find sources:</i>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&amp;q=%22Guinea%22">"Guinea"</a>&#160;–&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&amp;q=%22Guinea%22+-wikipedia&amp;tbs=ar:1">news</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?&amp;q=%22Guinea%22&amp;tbs=bkt:s&amp;tbm=bks">newspapers</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&amp;q=%22Guinea%22+-wikipedia">books</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Guinea%22">scholar</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Guinea%22&amp;acc=on&amp;wc=on">JSTOR</a></span></small></span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">September 2018</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>The land that is now Guinea belonged to a series of African empires until France colonized it in the 1890s, and made it part of <a href="/wiki/French_West_Africa" title="French West Africa">French West Africa</a>. Guinea declared its independence from France on 2 October 1958. From independence until the <a href="/wiki/Guinean_presidential_election,_2010" class="mw-redirect" title="Guinean presidential election, 2010">presidential election</a> of 2010, Guinea was governed by a number of <a href="/wiki/Autocracy" title="Autocracy">autocratic</a> rulers.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="West_African_empires_and_kingdoms">West African empires and kingdoms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: West African empires and kingdoms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Imamate_of_Futa_Jallon" title="Imamate of Futa Jallon">Imamate of Futa Jallon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Wassoulou_Empire" title="Wassoulou Empire">Wassoulou Empire</a></div>
<p>What is now Guinea was on the fringes of some West African empires. The earliest, the <a href="/wiki/Ghana_Empire" title="Ghana Empire">Ghana Empire</a>, grew on trade and ultimately fell after repeated incursions of the <a href="/wiki/Almoravids" class="mw-redirect" title="Almoravids">Almoravids</a>. It was in this period that Islam first arrived in the region by way of North African traders.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Sosso_Empire" title="Sosso Empire">Sosso Empire</a> (12th to 13th centuries) flourished in the resulting void, and the <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali Empire</a> came to prominence when <a href="/wiki/Sundiata_Keita" title="Sundiata Keita">Soundiata Kéïta</a> defeated the Sosso ruler <a href="/wiki/Soumaoro_Kant%C3%A9" title="Soumaoro Kanté">Soumangourou Kanté</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kirina" title="Battle of Kirina">Battle of Kirina</a>, in <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1235</span>. The Mali Empire was ruled by <a href="/wiki/Mansa_(title)" title="Mansa (title)">Mansa</a> (Emperors), including <a href="/wiki/Musa_I_of_Mali" class="mw-redirect" title="Musa I of Mali">Kankou Moussa</a> who made a <a href="/wiki/Hajj" title="Hajj">hajj</a> to Mecca in 1324. After his reign, the Mali Empire began to decline and was ultimately supplanted by its <a href="/wiki/Vassal" title="Vassal">vassal</a> states in the 15th century.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Songhai_Empire" title="Songhai Empire">Songhai Empire</a> expanded its power from about 1460 and eventually surpassed the Mali Empire in both territory and wealth. It continued to prosper until a civil war, over succession, followed the death of <a href="/wiki/Askia_Daoud" title="Askia Daoud">Askia Daoud</a> in 1582. The empire fell to invaders from <a href="/wiki/Morocco" title="Morocco">Morocco</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tondibi" title="Battle of Tondibi">Battle of Tondibi</a> 3 years later. The Moroccans proved unable to rule the kingdom effectively and it split into smaller kingdoms.
</p><p>After the fall of some of the West African empires, various kingdoms existed in what is now Guinea. <a href="/wiki/Fulani" class="mw-redirect" title="Fulani">Fulani</a> Muslims migrated to <a href="/wiki/Futa_Jallon" class="mw-redirect" title="Futa Jallon">Futa Jallon</a> in Central Guinea, and established an Islamic state from 1727 to 1896, with a written constitution and alternate rulers. The <a href="/wiki/Wassoulou_Empire" title="Wassoulou Empire">Wassoulou or Wassulu Empire</a> (1878–1898) was led by <a href="/wiki/Samori_Ture" title="Samori Ture">Samori Toure</a> in the predominantly <a href="/wiki/Mandinka_people" title="Mandinka people">Malinké</a> area of what is now upper Guinea and southwestern <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a> (Wassoulou). It moved to <a href="/wiki/Ivory_Coast" title="Ivory Coast">Ivory Coast</a> before being conquered by the French.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Colony">Colony</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Colony">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>European traders competed for the cape trade from the 17th century onward and made inroads earlier.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Slaves were exported to work elsewhere. The traders used the regional slave practices.
</p><p>Guinea's colonial period began with French military penetration into the area in the mid-19th century. French domination was assured by the defeat in 1898 of the armies of <a href="/wiki/Samori_Tour%C3%A9" class="mw-redirect" title="Samori Touré">Samori Touré</a>, Mansa (or Emperor) of the <a href="/wiki/Ouassoulou" class="mw-redirect" title="Ouassoulou">Ouassoulou</a> state and leader of Malinké descent, which gave France control of what today is Guinea and adjacent areas.
</p><p>France negotiated Guinea's present boundaries in the late 19th and early 20th centuries with the British for <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone" title="Sierra Leone">Sierra Leone</a>, the Portuguese for their Guinea colony (now <a href="/wiki/Guinea-Bissau" title="Guinea-Bissau">Guinea-Bissau</a>), and <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a>. Under the French, the country formed the <a href="/wiki/French_Guinea" title="French Guinea">Territory of Guinea</a> within <a href="/wiki/French_West_Africa" title="French West Africa">French West Africa</a>, administered by a governor general resident in <a href="/wiki/Dakar" title="Dakar">Dakar</a>. Lieutenant governors administered the individual colonies, including Guinea.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9_na_obisku_v_Ljubljani_1961_(3).jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9_na_obisku_v_Ljubljani_1961_%283%29.jpg/220px-Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9_na_obisku_v_Ljubljani_1961_%283%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="143" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9_na_obisku_v_Ljubljani_1961_%283%29.jpg/330px-Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9_na_obisku_v_Ljubljani_1961_%283%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9_na_obisku_v_Ljubljani_1961_%283%29.jpg/440px-Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9_na_obisku_v_Ljubljani_1961_%283%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1732" data-file-height="1126" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9_na_obisku_v_Ljubljani_1961_(3).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>President <a href="/wiki/Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9" title="Ahmed Sékou Touré">Ahmed Sékou Touré</a> was supported by Communist states, and in 1961, visited <a href="/wiki/Yugoslavia" title="Yugoslavia">Yugoslavia</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>In 1958, the <a href="/wiki/French_Fourth_Republic" title="French Fourth Republic">French Fourth Republic</a> collapsed due to political instability and its failures in dealing with its colonies, especially <a href="/wiki/Indochina" class="mw-redirect" title="Indochina">Indochina</a> and <a href="/wiki/Algeria" title="Algeria">Algeria</a>. The <a href="/wiki/French_Fifth_Republic" title="French Fifth Republic">French Fifth Republic</a> gave the colonies the choice of <a href="/wiki/Autonomous_entity" class="mw-redirect" title="Autonomous entity">autonomy</a> in a new <a href="/wiki/French_Community" title="French Community">French Community</a> or immediate independence, in the <a href="/wiki/1958_Guinean_constitutional_referendum" title="1958 Guinean constitutional referendum">referendum</a> of 28 September 1958. Unlike most other colonies, Guinea voted overwhelmingly for independence. It was led by <a href="/wiki/Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9" title="Ahmed Sékou Touré">Ahmed Sékou Touré</a> whose <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Guinea-African_Democratic_Rally" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic Party of Guinea-African Democratic Rally">Democratic Party of Guinea-African Democratic Rally</a> (PDG) had won 56 of 60 seats in 1957 territorial elections. The French withdrew, and on 2 October 1958, Guinea proclaimed itself a sovereign and independent republic, with Sékou Touré as president.
</p><p><i>The Washington Post</i> observed the "brutal" French tearing down all that they considered their contributions to Guinea: "In reaction, and as a warning to other French-speaking territories, the French pulled out of Guinea over a two-month period, taking everything they could with them. They unscrewed lightbulbs, removed plans for sewage pipelines in Conakry, the capital, and even burned medicines rather than leave them for the Guineans."<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Post-colonial_.281958.E2.80.93.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-colonial_(1958–)">Post-colonial (1958–)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Post-colonial (1958–)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"/><table class="box-More_citations_needed plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Guinea&amp;action=edit">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.<br /><small><span class="plainlinks"><i>Find sources:</i>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&amp;q=%22Guinea%22">"Guinea"</a>&#160;–&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&amp;q=%22Guinea%22+-wikipedia&amp;tbs=ar:1">news</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?&amp;q=%22Guinea%22&amp;tbs=bkt:s&amp;tbm=bks">newspapers</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&amp;q=%22Guinea%22+-wikipedia">books</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Guinea%22">scholar</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Guinea%22&amp;acc=on&amp;wc=on">JSTOR</a></span></small></span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">September 2021</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Op%C3%A9ration_Persil" title="Opération Persil">Opération Persil</a></div>
<p>Subsequently, Guinea aligned itself with the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a> and adopted <a href="/wiki/Socialism" title="Socialism">socialist</a> policies. It then moved towards a Chinese model of socialism. It continued to receive investment from <a href="/wiki/Capitalism" title="Capitalism">capitalist</a> countries, such as the United States. By 1960, Touré had declared PDG the country's only legal political party, and for the next 24 years, the government and PDG were one. Touré was re-elected unopposed to 4 7-year terms as president, and every 5 years voters were presented with a single list of PDG candidates for the National Assembly. Advocating a hybrid <a href="/wiki/African_Socialism" class="mw-redirect" title="African Socialism">African Socialism</a> domestically and <a href="/wiki/Pan-Africanism" title="Pan-Africanism">Pan-Africanism</a> abroad, Touré became a polarising leader, with his government becoming intolerant of dissent, imprisoning thousands, and stifling the press.
</p><p>Throughout the 1960s, the Guinean government nationalised land, removed French-appointed and traditional chiefs from power, and had strained ties with the French government and French companies. Touré's government relied on the Soviet Union and China for infrastructure aid and development, and much of this was used for political and not economic purposes, such as the building of stadiums to hold political rallies.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:22novemberdetail.JPG" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/22novemberdetail.JPG/220px-22novemberdetail.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="362" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/22novemberdetail.JPG/330px-22novemberdetail.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/22novemberdetail.JPG/440px-22novemberdetail.JPG 2x" data-file-width="455" data-file-height="749" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:22novemberdetail.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Monument to commemorate the 1970 military victory over the Portuguese raid. The key objective not accomplished by the Portuguese raid was the capture of <a href="/wiki/Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9" title="Ahmed Sékou Touré">Ahmed Sékou Touré</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>On 22 November 1970, Portuguese forces from neighbouring <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Guinea" title="Portuguese Guinea">Portuguese Guinea</a> staged <a href="/wiki/Operation_Green_Sea" title="Operation Green Sea">Operation Green Sea</a>, a raid on Conakry by several hundred exiled Guinean opposition forces. Among their goals, the Portuguese military wanted to kill or capture Sekou Touré due to his support of <a href="/wiki/PAIGC" class="mw-redirect" title="PAIGC">PAIGC</a>, an independence movement and rebel group that had carried out attacks inside Portuguese Guinea from their bases in Guinea.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> After some fighting, the Portuguese-backed forces retreated, having freed several dozen Portuguese prisoners of war that were being held by PAIGC in Conakry, and without having ousted Touré. In the years after the raid, purges were carried out by the Touré government, and at least 50 thousand people were killed. Others were imprisoned and faced torture. Some foreigners were forced to leave the country, after having had their Guinean spouse arrested and their children placed into state custody.
</p><p>Guinea was elected as a non-permanent member of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council" title="United Nations Security Council">UN Security Council</a> 1972–73.
</p><p>In 1977, a declining economy, mass killings, a stifling political atmosphere, and a ban on all private economic transactions led to the <a href="/wiki/Guinean_Market_Women%27s_Revolt" title="Guinean Market Women&#39;s Revolt">Market Women's Revolt</a>, a series of anti-government riots started by women working in Conakry's <a href="/wiki/March%C3%A9_Madina_(Conakry)" title="Marché Madina (Conakry)">Madina Market</a>. This prompted Touré to make major reforms. Touré vacillated from supporting the Soviet Union to supporting the United States. The late 1970s and early 1980s saw some economic reforms, while Touré's centralized control of the state remained. In the relationship with France, after the election of <a href="/wiki/Val%C3%A9ry_Giscard_d%27Estaing" title="Valéry Giscard d&#39;Estaing">Valéry Giscard d'Estaing</a> as French president, trade increased and the 2 countries exchanged diplomatic visits.
</p><p>Sékou Touré died on 26 March 1984, after a heart operation in the United States, and was replaced by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Louis_Lansana_Beavogui" title="Louis Lansana Beavogui">Louis Lansana Beavogui</a>, who was to serve as interim president, pending new elections. PDG was due to elect a new leader on 3 April 1984. Under the constitution, that person would have been the only candidate for president. Hours before that meeting, Colonels <a href="/wiki/Lansana_Cont%C3%A9" title="Lansana Conté">Lansana Conté</a> and <a href="/wiki/Diarra_Traor%C3%A9" title="Diarra Traoré">Diarra Traoré</a> seized power in a bloodless coup. Conté assumed the role of president, with Traoré serving as prime minister, until December.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Jimmy_Carter_with_President_Ahmed_S%C7%A9ou_Tour_%CC%8Cof_Guinea.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/President_Jimmy_Carter_with_President_Ahmed_S%C7%A9ou_Tour_%CC%8Cof_Guinea.jpg/220px-President_Jimmy_Carter_with_President_Ahmed_S%C7%A9ou_Tour_%CC%8Cof_Guinea.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="148" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/President_Jimmy_Carter_with_President_Ahmed_S%C7%A9ou_Tour_%CC%8Cof_Guinea.jpg/330px-President_Jimmy_Carter_with_President_Ahmed_S%C7%A9ou_Tour_%CC%8Cof_Guinea.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/President_Jimmy_Carter_with_President_Ahmed_S%C7%A9ou_Tour_%CC%8Cof_Guinea.jpg/440px-President_Jimmy_Carter_with_President_Ahmed_S%C7%A9ou_Tour_%CC%8Cof_Guinea.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4559" data-file-height="3072" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Jimmy_Carter_with_President_Ahmed_S%C7%A9ou_Tour_%CC%8Cof_Guinea.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Jimmy_Carter" title="Jimmy Carter">Jimmy Carter</a> welcoming <a href="/wiki/Ahmed_S%C3%A9kou_Tour%C3%A9" title="Ahmed Sékou Touré">Ahmed Sékou Touré</a> outside the White House, Washington, D.C., 1979</div></div></div>
<p>Conté denounced the previous regime's record on human rights, releasing 250 political prisoners and encouraging approximately 200 thousand more to return from exile. He made explicit the turn away from socialism.
</p><p>In 1992, Conté announced a return to civilian rule, with a presidential poll in 1993, followed by elections to parliament in 1995 (in which his party—the <a href="/wiki/Party_of_Unity_and_Progress" class="mw-redirect" title="Party of Unity and Progress">Party of Unity and Progress</a>—won 71 of 114 seats.) In September 2001, the opposition leader <a href="/wiki/Alpha_Cond%C3%A9" title="Alpha Condé">Alpha Condé</a> was imprisoned for endangering state security and pardoned 8 months later. Subsequently, he spent time in exile in France.
</p><p>In 2001, Conté organized and won a referendum to lengthen the presidential term, and in 2003, began his third term after elections were boycotted by the opposition. In January 2005, Conté survived a suspected assassination attempt while making a public appearance in the capital of <a href="/wiki/Conakry" title="Conakry">Conakry</a>. His opponents claimed that he was a "tired dictator",<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> whose departure was inevitable, whereas his supporters believed that he was winning a battle with dissidents. According to <i><a href="/wiki/Foreign_Policy" title="Foreign Policy">Foreign Policy</a></i>, Guinea was in danger of becoming a <a href="/wiki/Failed_state" title="Failed state">failed state</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2000, Guinea suffered from the instability which had blighted the rest of West Africa, as rebels crossed the borders with <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone" title="Sierra Leone">Sierra Leone</a>. It seemed that the country was headed for civil war.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Conté blamed neighbouring leaders for coveting Guinea's natural resources, and these claims were denied.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> In 2003, Guinea agreed to plans with her neighbours to tackle the insurgents. The <a href="/wiki/2007_Guinean_general_strike" title="2007 Guinean general strike">2007 Guinean general strike</a> resulted in the appointment of a new prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Conté remained in power until his death on 23 December 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Several hours after his death, <a href="/wiki/Moussa_Dadis_Camara" title="Moussa Dadis Camara">Moussa Dadis Camara</a> seized control in a <a href="/wiki/2008_Guinean_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="2008 Guinean coup d&#39;état">coup</a>, declaring himself head of a <a href="/wiki/Military_junta" title="Military junta">military junta</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Protests against the coup became violent, and 157 people were killed when, on 28 September 2009, the junta ordered its soldiers to attack people gathered to protest Camara's attempt to become president.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The soldiers went on a rampage of rape, mutilation, and murder, which caused some foreign governments to withdraw their support for the new regime.<sup id="cite_ref-NYT_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 3 December 2009, an aide shot Camara during a dispute over the rampage in September. Camara went to Morocco for medical care.<sup id="cite_ref-NYT_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Vice-president (and defense minister) <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A9kouba_Konat%C3%A9" title="Sékouba Konaté">Sékouba Konaté</a> flew from <a href="/wiki/Lebanon" title="Lebanon">Lebanon</a> to run the country.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> After meeting in <a href="/wiki/Ouagadougou" title="Ouagadougou">Ouagadougou</a> on 13 and 14 January 2010, Camara, Konaté and <a href="/wiki/Blaise_Compaor%C3%A9" title="Blaise Compaoré">Blaise Compaoré</a>, President of <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a>, produced a formal statement of 12 principles promising a return of Guinea to civilian rule within 6 months.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> The presidential election of 27 June<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-transitional_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-transitional-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> brought allegations of fraud, and a second election was held on 7 November.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Voter turnout was "high", and the elections went "relatively smoothly".<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Alpha_Cond%C3%A9" title="Alpha Condé">Alpha Condé</a>, leader of the opposition party <a href="/wiki/Rally_of_the_Guinean_People" title="Rally of the Guinean People">Rally of the Guinean People</a> (RGP), won the election, promising to reform the security sector and review mining contracts.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
In February 2013, <a href="/wiki/2013_Guinea_Violence" class="mw-redirect" title="2013 Guinea Violence">political violence erupted</a> after street protests over transparency of upcoming May elections. The protests were fueled by the opposition coalition's decision to step down from the elections, in protest of the lack of transparency in the preparations for elections.<sup id="cite_ref-Rone_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rone-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> 9 people were killed during the protests, and around 220 were injured. Some deaths and injuries were caused by security forces using live ammunition on protesters.<sup id="cite_ref-Reuters_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reuters-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Rtwo_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rtwo-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> The violence led to ethnic clashes between the <a href="/wiki/Malinke" class="mw-redirect" title="Malinke">Malinke</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fula_people" title="Fula people">Fula</a>, who supported and opposed President Condé, respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> On 26 March 2013, the opposition party backed out of negotiations with the government over the election, saying that the government had not respected them, and had broken all agreements.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></p><div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:252px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Acte2_FNDC.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Acte2_FNDC.jpg/250px-Acte2_FNDC.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Acte2_FNDC.jpg/375px-Acte2_FNDC.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Acte2_FNDC.jpg/500px-Acte2_FNDC.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4128" data-file-height="3096" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Acte2_FNDC.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/2019%E2%80%932020_Guinean_protests" title="2019–2020 Guinean protests">2019–2020 Guinean protests</a> against the rule of <a href="/wiki/Alpha_Cond%C3%A9" title="Alpha Condé">Alpha Condé</a></div></div></div>
<p>On 25 March 2014, the <a href="/wiki/World_Health_Organization" title="World Health Organization">World Health Organization</a> stated that <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Health_(Guinea)" title="Ministry of Health (Guinea)">Guinea's Ministry of Health</a> had reported <a href="/wiki/Ebola_virus_epidemic_in_Guinea" title="Ebola virus epidemic in Guinea">an outbreak</a> of <a href="/wiki/Ebola_virus_disease" class="mw-redirect" title="Ebola virus disease">Ebola virus disease</a> in Guinea. This initial outbreak had 86 cases, including 59 deaths. By 28 May, there were 281 cases, with 186 deaths.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> It is believed that the first case was Emile Ouamouno, a 2-year-old boy in the village of <a href="/wiki/Meliandou" title="Meliandou">Meliandou</a>. He fell ill on 2 December 2013 and died on 6 December.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> On 18 September 2014, 8 members of an Ebola education health care team <a href="/wiki/Womey_Massacre" class="mw-redirect" title="Womey Massacre">were murdered</a> by villagers in the town of <a href="/wiki/Womey" title="Womey">Womey</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> As of 1 November 2015, there had been 3,810 cases and 2,536 deaths in Guinea.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/2019%E2%80%932020_Guinean_protests" title="2019–2020 Guinean protests">2019–2020 Guinean protests</a> were a series of violent protests and mass civil unrest against the rule of Alpha Conde that broke out on October 14, 2019, against constitutional changes. More than 800 were killed in clashes.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> After the <a href="/wiki/2020_Guinean_presidential_election" title="2020 Guinean presidential election">2020 Guinean presidential election</a>, Alpha Condé's election to a third term was challenged by the opposition, who accused him of fraud. Condé claimed a <a href="/wiki/2020_Guinean_constitutional_referendum" title="2020 Guinean constitutional referendum">constitutional referendum from March 2020</a> allowed him to run despite the 2-term limit.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 5 September 2021, after hours of gunfire near the presidential palace, Lieutenant Colonel <a href="/wiki/Mamady_Doumbouya" title="Mamady Doumbouya">Mamady Doumbouya</a> seized control of state television and declared that President <a href="/wiki/Alpha_Cond%C3%A9" title="Alpha Condé">Alpha Conde</a>'s government had been dissolved and the nation's borders closed.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> By the evening, the putschists declared control of all Conakry and the country's armed forces. According to <i>Guinée Matin,</i> by 6 September the military fully controlled the state administration and started to replace the civil administration with its military counterpart.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>, <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>, <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a>, <a href="/wiki/ECOWAS" class="mw-redirect" title="ECOWAS">ECOWAS</a> (which suspended Guinea's membership) and <a href="/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie" title="Organisation internationale de la Francophonie">La Francophonie</a> denounced the coup, and called for President Condé's unconditional release. Similar responses came from some neighboring and Western countries (including the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-leaders_due_in_guinea_2021_09_09_reuters_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leaders_due_in_guinea_2021_09_09_reuters-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-leaders_suspend_guinea_2021_09_08_bbc_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leaders_suspend_guinea_2021_09_08_bbc-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-china_is_ok_2021_09_foreign_policy_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-china_is_ok_2021_09_foreign_policy-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> and from <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">China</a> (which relies on Guinea for half of its aluminum ore, facilitated by its connections to President Condé).<sup id="cite_ref-china_is_ok_2021_09_foreign_policy_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-china_is_ok_2021_09_foreign_policy-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Despite these, On 1 October 2021, Mamady Doumbouya was sworn in as interim President.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Guinea" title="Geography of Guinea">Geography of Guinea</a></div>
<div class="center"><div class="thumb tnone"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:552px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Un-guinea.png" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Un-guinea.png/550px-Un-guinea.png" decoding="async" width="550" height="421" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Un-guinea.png/825px-Un-guinea.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Un-guinea.png/1100px-Un-guinea.png 2x" data-file-width="3092" data-file-height="2367" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Un-guinea.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A map showing <a href="/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Guinea" title="List of cities in Guinea">Guinea's cities</a> and <a href="/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Guinea" class="mw-redirect" title="Administrative divisions of Guinea">administrative divisions</a></div></div></div></div>
<p>Guinea shares a border with <a href="/wiki/Guinea-Bissau" title="Guinea-Bissau">Guinea-Bissau</a> to <a href="/wiki/Guinea%E2%80%93Guinea-Bissau_border" title="Guinea–Guinea-Bissau border">the northwest</a>, <a href="/wiki/Senegal" title="Senegal">Senegal</a> to <a href="/wiki/Guinea%E2%80%93Senegal_border" title="Guinea–Senegal border">the north</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a> to <a href="/wiki/Guinea%E2%80%93Mali_border" title="Guinea–Mali border">the northeast</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ivory_Coast" title="Ivory Coast">Ivory Coast</a> to <a href="/wiki/Guinea%E2%80%93Ivory_Coast_border" title="Guinea–Ivory Coast border">the east</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone" title="Sierra Leone">Sierra Leone</a> to <a href="/wiki/Guinea%E2%80%93Sierra_Leone_border" title="Guinea–Sierra Leone border">the southwest</a> and <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a> to <a href="/wiki/Guinea%E2%80%93Liberia_border" title="Guinea–Liberia border">the south</a>. The nation forms a crescent as it curves from its southeast region to the north and west, to its northwest border with <a href="/wiki/Guinea-Bissau" title="Guinea-Bissau">Guinea-Bissau</a> and southwestern coast on the Atlantic Ocean. The sources of the <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger River</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Gambia_River" title="Gambia River">Gambia River</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Senegal_River" title="Senegal River">Senegal River</a> are all found in the <a href="/wiki/Guinea_Highlands" title="Guinea Highlands">Guinea Highlands</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> At 245,857&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (94,926&#160;sq&#160;mi), Guinea is roughly the size of the United Kingdom. There are 320&#160;km (200&#160;mi) of coastline and a total land border of 3,400&#160;km (2,100&#160;mi). It lies mostly between latitudes <a href="/wiki/7th_parallel_north" title="7th parallel north">7°</a> and <a href="/wiki/13th_parallel_north" title="13th parallel north">13°N</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/7th_meridian_west" title="7th meridian west">7°</a> and <a href="/wiki/15th_meridian_west" title="15th meridian west">15°W</a>, with a smaller area that is west of 15°.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_GIN_present.svg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Koppen-Geiger_Map_GIN_present.svg/220px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_GIN_present.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="142" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Koppen-Geiger_Map_GIN_present.svg/330px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_GIN_present.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Koppen-Geiger_Map_GIN_present.svg/440px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_GIN_present.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1321" data-file-height="850" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_GIN_present.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Map of Köppen climate classification</div></div></div>
<p>Guinea is divided into 4 regions: <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Guinea" title="Maritime Guinea">Maritime Guinea</a>, also known as Lower Guinea or the Basse-Coté lowlands, populated mainly by the <a href="/wiki/Susu_people" title="Susu people">Susu</a> ethnic group; the cooler, more mountainous <a href="/wiki/Fouta_Djallon" title="Fouta Djallon">Fouta Djallon</a> that run roughly north–south through the middle of the country, populated by Fulas; the Sahelian Haute-Guinea to the northeast, populated by <a href="/wiki/Mandinka_people" title="Mandinka people">Malinké</a>; and the forested jungle regions in the southeast, with several ethnic groups. Guinea's mountains are the source for the Niger, the Gambia, and Senegal Rivers, and rivers flowing to the sea on the west side of the range in Sierra Leone and Ivory Coast. The highest point in Guinea is <a href="/wiki/Mount_Nimba" class="mw-redirect" title="Mount Nimba">Mount Nimba</a> at 1,752&#160;m (5,748&#160;ft). While the Guinean and Ivorian sides of the <a href="/wiki/Nimba_Massif" class="mw-redirect" title="Nimba Massif">Nimba Massif</a> are a <a href="/wiki/UNESCO" title="UNESCO">UNESCO</a> <a href="/wiki/Strict_Nature_Reserve" class="mw-redirect" title="Strict Nature Reserve">Strict Nature Reserve</a>, the portion of the so-called Guinean Backbone continues into <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a>, where it has been mined for decades; the damage is evident in the <a href="/wiki/Nz%C3%A9r%C3%A9kor%C3%A9_Region" title="Nzérékoré Region">Nzérékoré Region</a> at <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1073938472"/><span class="plainlinks nourlexpansion"><a class="external text" href="//geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Guinea&amp;params=7_32_17_N_8_29_50_W_region:GN_type:landmark"><span class="geo-default"><span class="geo-dms" title="Maps, aerial photos, and other data for this location"><span class="latitude">7°32′17″N</span> <span class="longitude">8°29′50″W</span></span></span><span class="geo-multi-punct">&#xfeff; / &#xfeff;</span><span class="geo-nondefault"><span class="geo-dec" title="Maps, aerial photos, and other data for this location">7.53806°N 8.49722°W</span><span style="display:none">&#xfeff; / <span class="geo">7.53806; -8.49722</span></span></span></a></span>.
</p><p>Guinea is home to 5 ecoregions: <a href="/wiki/Guinean_montane_forests" title="Guinean montane forests">Guinean montane forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Western_Guinean_lowland_forests" title="Western Guinean lowland forests">Western Guinean lowland forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guinean_forest-savanna_mosaic" class="mw-redirect" title="Guinean forest-savanna mosaic">Guinean forest-savanna mosaic</a>, <a href="/wiki/West_Sudanian_savanna" title="West Sudanian savanna">West Sudanian savanna</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Guinean_mangroves" title="Guinean mangroves">Guinean mangroves</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DinersteinOlson2017_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> It had a 2019 <a href="/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index" title="Forest Landscape Integrity Index">Forest Landscape Integrity Index</a> mean score of 4.9/10, ranking it 114th globally out of 172 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FLII-Supplementary_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Wildlife">Wildlife</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Wildlife">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_of_Guinea" title="Wildlife of Guinea">Wildlife of Guinea</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:252px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Parc_national_Badiar.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Parc_national_Badiar.jpg/250px-Parc_national_Badiar.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="166" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Parc_national_Badiar.jpg/375px-Parc_national_Badiar.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Parc_national_Badiar.jpg/500px-Parc_national_Badiar.jpg 2x" data-file-width="624" data-file-height="415" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Parc_national_Badiar.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Badiar_National_Park" title="Badiar National Park">Badiar National Park</a></div></div></div>
<p>The southern part of Guinea lies within the <a href="/wiki/Guinean_Forests_of_West_Africa" title="Guinean Forests of West Africa">Guinean Forests of West Africa</a> <a href="/wiki/Biodiversity_hotspot" title="Biodiversity hotspot">Biodiversity hotspot</a>, while the north-east is characterized by dry savanna woodlands. Declining populations of some animals are restricted to uninhabited distant parts of parks and reserves.
</p><p>Species found in Guinea include the following:
</p>
<ul><li>Amphibians&#160;: <i><a href="/wiki/Hemisus_guineensis" class="mw-redirect" title="Hemisus guineensis">Hemisus guineensis</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Phrynobatrachus_guineensis" title="Phrynobatrachus guineensis">Phrynobatrachus guineensis</a></i></li>
<li>Reptiles&#160;: <i><a href="/wiki/Acanthodactylus" title="Acanthodactylus">Acanthodactylus</a> guineensis</i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Mochlus" title="Mochlus">Mochlus</a> guineensis</i></li>
<li>Arachnids: <i><a href="/wiki/Malloneta_guineensis" class="mw-redirect" title="Malloneta guineensis">Malloneta guineensis</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Dictyna" title="Dictyna">Dictyna</a> guineensis</i></li>
<li>Insects&#160;: <i><a href="/wiki/Zorotypus" title="Zorotypus">Zorotypus</a> guineensis</i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Euchromia_guineensis" title="Euchromia guineensis">Euchromia guineensis</a></i></li>
<li>Birds: <i><a href="/wiki/Melaniparus_guineensis" class="mw-redirect" title="Melaniparus guineensis">Melaniparus guineensis</a></i></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Regions_and_prefectures">Regions and prefectures</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Regions and prefectures">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Guinea" class="mw-redirect" title="Administrative divisions of Guinea">Administrative divisions of Guinea</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:352px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Guinea_Regions.png" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Guinea_Regions.png/350px-Guinea_Regions.png" decoding="async" width="350" height="285" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/1c/Guinea_Regions.png 1.5x" data-file-width="386" data-file-height="314" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Guinea_Regions.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Guinea" title="Regions of Guinea">Regions of Guinea</a></div></div></div>
<p>The Republic of Guinea covers 245,857 square kilometres (94,926&#160;sq&#160;mi) of West Africa, about <a href="/wiki/10th_parallel_north" title="10th parallel north">10 degrees north</a> of the equator. It is divided into 4 natural regions with distinct human, geographic, and climatic characteristics:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Maritime_Guinea" title="Maritime Guinea">Maritime Guinea</a> (<i>La Guinée Maritime</i>) covers 18% of the country.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Middle_Guinea" title="Middle Guinea">Middle Guinea</a> (<i>La Moyenne-Guinée</i>) covers 20% of the country.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Upper_Guinea" title="Upper Guinea">Upper Guinea</a> (<i>La Haute-Guinée</i>) covers 38% of the country.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Guin%C3%A9e_foresti%C3%A8re" title="Guinée forestière">Forested Guinea</a> (<i>Guinée forestière</i>) covers 23% of the country, and is both forested and mountainous.</li></ul>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:252px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Fouta_Djallon_(14582291826).jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Fouta_Djallon_%2814582291826%29.jpg/250px-Fouta_Djallon_%2814582291826%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Fouta_Djallon_%2814582291826%29.jpg/375px-Fouta_Djallon_%2814582291826%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Fouta_Djallon_%2814582291826%29.jpg/500px-Fouta_Djallon_%2814582291826%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Fouta_Djallon_(14582291826).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Fouta_Djallon" title="Fouta Djallon">Fouta Djallon</a> highlands in central Guinea</div></div></div>
<p>Guinea is divided into 8 <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Guinea" title="Regions of Guinea">administrative regions</a> which are subdivided into 33 <a href="/wiki/Prefectures_of_Guinea" title="Prefectures of Guinea">prefectures</a>. The capital <a href="/wiki/Conakry" title="Conakry">Conakry</a> with a population of 1,675,069 ranks as a special zone.
</p>
